\subsection{Why assessments and Why regressivity? }
Refs:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \href{https://www.nyc.gov/assets/finance/jump/property-data-maps/docs/quintos_equity_in_mass_appraisal.pdf?utm_source=perplexity}{Improving Assessment Equity in Mass Appraisal Models}
\end{enumerate}

Assessment equity is a primary concern for municipalities for taxes purposes; the major concerns being the assessment levels
to be fair for properties across price levels \textbf{(vertical equity)} and across properties with similar characteristics \textbf{(horizontal equity)} \red{(Check IAAO Chapter 8)}. The International Association of Assessing Officers defines different standard measures for horizontal and vertical equity, for example, the coefficient of dispersion (COD) and the vertical equity indicator (VEI, \href{https://www.iaao.org/about/board-of-directors/governing-documents/ratio-studies-exposure-draft/}{since 2025}) [price-related differential (PRD) previously], respectively \red{(see 1)}. According to \red{(1)}, the IAAO range for COD is 5 to 15 percent for single-family homes, and the range for the VEI is -10 to 10 percent [for the PRD is 0.98-1.03]. Assessors usually build confidence intervals with bootstrap techniques for securing those levels in a statistically significant manner. although they seem to fail when heteroskedasticity (variance of the residuals is not constant across certain property characteristics \red{[e.g. price?]} in the case of horizontal equity and unexplained heterogeneity, i.e. heteroskedastic residuals, for vertical equity) is present. The main problem of identifying the heterogeneity of the parcels is that unsold ones need to be assessed (the market prices are unknown), and these estimations need to maintain consistency; for example, similar properties in the same block/area/cluster should be assigned similar prices. 

\todo{I am missing something about consequences and impact of taxes here, or at least some references.}


\subsection{Case of Study: Cook County Assessor's Office}

We will focus this work on the case of study of the Cook County Assessor's Office (CCAO). The CCAO has an extensive, open-access, repository \red{(REPO)} with the code, analysis, and explanations of  the assessments (Mass Appraisal) of house prices in the Cook County from 2009 to 2025 (continuously growing). From the years 2009 to 2017, they utilized strictly linear models (linear regression) as their assessment machine learning model, and moved towards nonlinear  model ensembles (Gradient Boosting-based) starting on 2019. In the last years, they have been solely focusing on their work with one of the state-of-the-art methods; the LightGBM library \red{(ref)}, which is a Gradient Boosting implementation with loss second order geometry awareness (Newton Method-like). 

Although powerful tools for inference, machine learning models are usually black boxes, which makes their inference results hard to interpret, specially in sensitive context as the mass appraisal problem \red{(ref)}. Furthermore, given the nature of the problem, these problems usually aim only to improve performance in prediction error terms, which makes them adjust to the average values and where the majority of the mass of data-points is concentrates, resulting in models that over-predict low-value properties while under-predicting high-valued ones, which is a vertical inequity behavior (also known as regressivity). An example of this behavior on the dataset used by CCAO on 2023 \red{check} and using LightGBM as the predictive model is shown in Figure \ref{fig:regressive_example}, which shows the comparison of log-prices ($\log(Y)$) of the properties versus the ratio-over-assessments, defined as
$$
RtA(X, Y) = \frac{A(X)}{Y},
$$
where $A(X)$ is our prediction of the property values, and $Y$ is the real market value of the same properties (assuming we already have access to that data). In a perfect model (or an over-fitted one), that achieves $A(X)=Y$, $RtA$ is always equal to one. However, one can observe in the real example on the Figure, that the linear tendency (red line) is not constant $RtA=1$, but it has a negative slope (decreasing tendency). This is, the low-value houses are indeed being under-predicted in average ($RtA>1$) while higher-value properties are being under-predicted in average $RtA<1$), which is what we call a regressive behavior.


\begin{figure}[!h]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{Fairness Price/img/motivation/log_ratio_LGBMRegressor.png}
    \caption{Example of regressive behavior of LightGBM regressor results in test set \red{(Change it to a simpler one with names)}.}
    \label{fig:regressive_example}
\end{figure}

The statistical and financial consequences of this regressive behavior for the same dataset/model are summarized in Listing~\ref{lst:log}. The report is structured in three main blocks:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Model accuracy (price scale + log scale):} This block reports standard predictive accuracy metrics (MAE, RMSE, MAPE) in the original dollar scale, alongside error summaries in log-ratio space. These quantify overall predictive performance, but by themselves do not diagnose systematic price-related bias.

    \item \textbf{IAAO-style ratio study metrics (Pred/Actual as AV/SP):} This block evaluates assessment quality through the lens of ratio studies, where the basic object is the ratio $A/Y$. The \emph{median ratio} and \emph{weighted mean ratio} summarize the overall assessment level (how close assessments are to market values in aggregate). In the baseline, both are within the broad level range $[0.90,1.10]$, suggesting that the system is not dramatically mis-scaled overall. However, the equity indicators point to substantial problems: COD is $21.73\%$ (above the 5--15\% IAAO range), indicating large dispersion of ratios even after controlling for the general assessment level (horizontal inequity). More importantly for this work, PRD is $1.0795$ (above the 0.98--1.03 range) and VEI is $-29.02\%$ (outside the $[-10,10]\%$ range), both indicating a strong regressive tendency. \red{(Explain more the table)}

    \item \textbf{Diagnostics by price quantile:} This block makes the source of regressivity explicit by stratifying properties into quantiles of the \emph{actual} price $Y$. The first quantile (lowest-value homes) has mean ratio $1.189$ and median ratio $1.097$, while the highest quantile has mean ratio $0.891$ and median ratio $0.875$. This monotone decline in ratios across price groups is the empirical signature that drives PRD/VEI away from their acceptable ranges.
\end{itemize}

% \subsection{Approach, and why this approach?}


\subsection{Approach, and why this approach?}

\subsubsection{Approach}

First, note that if the deviation from 1 of the $RtA$ is zero, then we have no regressivity. Since we aim for an average ratio of 1, then we would expect that a model that explains perfectly the assessed prices would have $Var(RtA)\approx 0$. From the law of total variance we get that
\[
Var(RtA) \;=\; Var(\Expected{RtA\mid Y}) \;+\; \Expected{Var(RtA\mid Y)}.
\]
Note that this decomposition is useful because it separates a \emph{systematic} component (how the average ratio changes with the price level) from an \emph{idiosyncratic} component (dispersion of ratios among properties at a comparable price level).

More precisely, write
\[
m(Y) := \mathbb{E}[RtA\mid Y], \qquad \varepsilon := RtA - m(Y),
\]
so that $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\mid Y]=0$. Then the first term $Var(m(Y))$ captures how much the \emph{average} assessment ratio varies across the value distribution. This is naturally linked to \textbf{vertical equity}: in an ideal vertically-equitable system, we would have
\[
m(Y)\approx 1 \ \text{for all }Y \quad \Longrightarrow \quad Var(\mathbb{E}[RtA\mid Y])\approx 0,
\]
which rules out regressivity patterns such as ``low-value homes over-assessed, high-value homes under-assessed.''

The second term $\mathbb{E}[Var(RtA\mid Y)] = \mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^2]$ measures the \emph{typical dispersion} of ratios around their local mean at each price level. This term is naturally linked to \textbf{horizontal equity} in the sense of ``similar-value homes should have similar ratios.'' This is closely related to IAAO dispersion measures (e.g., COD), which quantify the spread of ratios within strata.

Importantly, the standard ``overall level'' condition (median ratio or weighted mean ratio near 1) is not sufficient to guarantee vertical equity: it is possible to have $\mathbb{E}[RtA]\approx 1$ while $m(Y)$ is decreasing in $Y$ (regressivity), so that over-assessments at the bottom are offset by under-assessments at the top.

\vspace{0.5em}
\noindent \textbf{Connection to the covariance-based constraints/penalties.}
A convenient proxy for the vertical term is the covariance between the ratio and the market value:
\[
\mathrm{Cov}(RtA, Y) \;=\; \mathrm{Cov}\!\big(\mathbb{E}[RtA\mid Y],\, Y\big),
\]
since $\varepsilon$ is mean-zero conditional on $Y$ and therefore does not contribute to the covariance with $Y$. Thus, if $m(Y)$ has a negative trend (regressive behavior), $\mathrm{Cov}(RtA,Y)$ becomes negative (and its magnitude increases as the trend strengthens). This is the motivation for controlling regressivity by directly constraining or penalizing a covariance-type statistic between the ratio and $Y$ (or a standardized version such as $\mathrm{Cov}(RtA,Y)/\mathrm{Std}(Y)$), which is exactly the mechanism used in our covariance-regularized objectives.

% \vspace{0.5em}
% \noindent \textbf{Operationalization via binning/stratification (IAAO-style).}
% Because $Y$ is continuous and the sample is imbalanced across price levels, we approximate $\mathbb{E}[RtA\mid Y]$ and $Var(RtA\mid Y)$ by grouping observations into price strata (e.g., quantiles or equal-width bins) and computing within-bin summaries. This mirrors how ratio studies are conducted in practice and stabilizes inference under heteroskedasticity. In particular, equal-weighting strata (rather than weighting by the number of observations) avoids having the densest region of the price distribution dominate the equity objective.

% \vspace{0.5em}
% \noindent \textbf{Extending ``horizontal'' beyond price levels.}
% Conditioning on $Y$ captures dispersion among similar-value homes, but horizontal equity is often defined for ``similar characteristics'' more broadly \red{(Check IAAO Chapter 8)}. A more faithful version replaces or augments $Y$ with a similarity grouping $G(X)$ (e.g., neighborhood/market area/cluster of comparable properties):
% \[
% Var(RtA) = Var(\mathbb{E}[RtA\mid Y, G]) + \mathbb{E}[Var(RtA\mid Y, G)].
% \]
% This refinement separates price-related systematic effects (vertical) from within-comparable-group dispersion (horizontal), and it also aligns with the practical constraint that unsold parcels must be treated consistently within their comparable groups.

\red{Note: connect this to COD/PRD/VEI more explicitly, and to consequences/impact of taxes.}


\subsubsection{More about fairness in ML}


Machine learning (ML) models are increasingly used to support or automate high-stakes decisions in various domains, including credit, hiring, healthcare, and public policy (see \cite{Sahoh2023}). In these settings, optimizing predictive accuracy alone is not sufficient: stakeholders also care about whether model outputs systematically disadvantage certain individuals or groups. As a result, \emph{fairness} constraints have become a central requirement for responsible ML deployment, alongside traditional considerations like accuracy, interpretability, privacy, and robustness (see \cite{Mehrabi2021}).
\begin{figure}[!h]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth, trim={0 1cm 0 1cm}]{Fairness Price/MLOpt Project on Fairness/img/tradeoff_plot (1).pdf}
    \caption{Caption}
    \label{fig:tradeoff}
\end{figure}
A key difficulty is that fairness is inherently \emph{context-dependent}. Different applications, institutions, and legal frameworks can imply different (and sometimes incompatible) notions of fairness. For example, one may want to equalize error rates across protected groups, ensure that predictions are statistically independent of sensitive attributes, or avoid systematic regressivity in outcomes. This plurality of definitions means that fairness cannot be treated as a single universal scalar metric; rather, it is best viewed as a family of constraints and objectives that encode the values and requirements of a particular decision context. Further, the fairness measure are usually not even compatible with the accuracy of the model (e.g. \cite{Dutta2019ki}, \cite{Liu2020AccuracyAF}), as we can see in the \textit{tradeoff} curve between prediction error and unfairness in terms of pairwise difference of accuracy in Figure \ref{fig:tradeoff}.

% \subsection{Where does unfairness come from?}
Fairness concerns arise from multiple sources. Bias can be introduced by the data (e.g., underrepresentation, measurement error, historical inequities reflected in labels) and also by the learning algorithm itself (e.g., inductive biases and optimization choices that amplify disparities). Moreover, practical data issues such as missingness and distribution shift can disproportionately affect specific groups, making it possible for a model to appear fair on a training dataset while violating fairness desiderata after deployment. Therefore, addressing fairness requires an end-to-end perspective that goes beyond post-hoc evaluation.

These considerations motivate an \emph{optimization lens} on fairness in ML. Many fairness interventions can be naturally expressed as modifications to the learning problem:
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(i)] \emph{pre-processing} approaches that alter or reweight training data,
    \item[(ii)] \emph{in-processing} approaches that incorporate fairness directly into training via constraints, penalties, or multi-objective optimization (MOO) formulations, and
    \item[(iii)] \emph{post-processing} approaches that adjust outputs to satisfy fairness criteria.
\end{itemize}
Among these, in-processing and multi-objective perspectives are particularly attractive when fairness must be balanced against predictive performance and when multiple fairness desiderata are present simultaneously. Casting fairness as constraints or explicit objectives makes the trade-offs transparent and controllable, and it enables the use of modern optimization tools to analyze feasibility, sensitivity to hyperparameters, and the structure of the accuracy--fairness frontier.

Finally, in many realistic deployments, the sensitive attribute is available for auditing and model development, but may be restricted or undesirable to use at prediction time. This setting highlights a fundamental tension: we may want predictions that do not depend on sensitive attributes, yet we still need access to those attributes to \emph{measure} disparities and to \emph{enforce} fairness requirements during training. Overall, the goal is to develop learning formulations that make fairness a first-class design principle---yielding models that achieve strong predictive performance while satisfying application-relevant fairness requirements and providing interpretable trade-offs for decision makers.

\newpage

\section{Methodology}

\subsection{Vertical Equity-Aware Formulation}


Given the data $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ with $y_i=\log(P_i)$, let $\mathcal H$ be a hypothesis class and
$f\in\mathcal H$ be the mapping that outputs the log-predictions $\hat y_i:=f(x_i)$. Let $\bar y:=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n y_i$ be the average log-price, let $g(\hat y_i,y_i)$ be some proxy of the ratios between assessments (predictions) and the real prices, and let $C_{g}(f):=\widehat{\text{Cov}}(g(\hat y_i,y_i), y)$ denote the empirical variance between the ratios proxy $g((\hat y_i,y_i)$ and the log-prices $y$ vectors. Let us also define the regularized baseline loss function $\mathcal{L}(f):=\frac{1}{2n}\|y-f(X)\|_2^2 + \Omega(f)$, where $\Omega$ is a model-dependent complexity regularizer of $f$.  

We are interested in solving a vertical equity (VE) aware problem, which makes the goal of our model a multi-objective one; we want to achieve the minimal loss value while maximizing the VE in some sense. As a first proxy of the VE, \todo{add references} and closely related to the IAAO metrics and definitions of VE, we solve the VE-aware covariance-constrained problem 
\begin{align}
    {\everymath{\displaystyle}
    \begin{array}{cccccc}
    (VE_{\varepsilon}):= &  \min_{f\in \H}  &  \L(f,y;X) & \text{s.t.} & C_g(f)^2 \leq \varepsilon,
    \end{array}
    }
    \label{opt:general_learner_constrained}
\end{align}
or the unconstrained penalized version of the problem  
\begin{align}
    {\everymath{\displaystyle}
    \begin{array}{ccccccc}
    (VE_{\rho}):= &  \min_{f\in \H}  &  \L(f,y;X) + \frac{\rho}{2} C_g(f)^2,
    \end{array}
    }
    \label{opt:general_learner_penalized}
\end{align}
where the equivalence between the two definitions depends on choosing the appropriate pair $(\varepsilon, \rho)$ that correspond to each other's solution.




% In theory, given a mapping $f:\X \rightarrow \Y$ that lies into some hypothesis space $\H$, and a loss function $\L:\H \times \R^n \times \R^{n\times p} \rightarrow \R$ that consist of the sum of an empirical risk measure $J$ and a complexity regularizer mapping $\Omega$ (i.e., $\L(f,y;X) := J(f,y;X) + \Omega(f)$), we are interested in solving the general learning problem of minimizing the loss function with $\varepsilon$-constrained vertical equity (EV) as
% \begin{align}
%     {\everymath{\displaystyle}
%     \begin{array}{crlcccc}
%     (VE_{\varepsilon}):= &  \min_{f\in \H}  &  \L(f,y;X) & s.t. & \left|\widehat{\text{Cov}}(g(r), y)\right| \leq \varepsilon,
%     \end{array}
%     }
%     \label{opt:general_learner}
% % \end{align}
% where \todo{to be defined properly in the options div/diff} $g(r)$ is some transformation of the ratios between the estimated vector $f(X)$ components and the real price vector $y$ components (i.e., $r_i = f(X)_i/y_i$), and  where we use the empirical covariance defined as $\widehat{\text{Cov}}(r,y) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (r_i - \Bar{r})(y_i - \Bar{y})= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n r_i(y_i - \Bar{y})$. We can define the transformation $g:\R^n \rightarrow \R^n$ in many ways, but for simplicity {\todo{Should I mention the empirical point?}(and because of its empirical performance)} we are going to constraint ourselves to the cases: (1) $g(r)_i=f(X)_i / y_i$, the direct ratio, and (2) $g(r)=f(X) - y$, that aims to constraint the the real prices ratios in cases where we are fitting the model in the log-price space. 


\subsection{Two ratio moments used in practice: division of log-prices and difference of log-prices}
\label{sec:diff_div}



\paragraph{Difference of log-prices (log assessment ratio).} 
Let the assessment ratios proxy $g$ in (\ref{opt:general_learner_constrained}) and (\ref{opt:general_learner_penalized}) be of the form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:g_diff}
g_{\texttt{diff}}(\hat y,y):=\hat y-y=\log(\hat P/P),
\end{equation}
where $\hat{P}/P$ is the real price assessment ratio. 
Then 
\todo{Note I am omitting the average term canceled-out}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_diff} 
C_{\texttt{diff}}(f)
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (\hat y_i-y_i)\,(y_i - \Bar{y})=
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \hat y_i\,(y_i - \Bar{y}) - \widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)=\widehat{\text{Cov}}(\hat{y}, y) - \widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y),
\end{equation}where the empirical covariance of the log-price is defined as $\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \Bar{y})^2$. Thus, if the model successfully imposes $C_{\texttt{diff}}(f)\approx 0$, then it is really enforcing equality in the second moment, i.e., $\text{Cov}(\hat{y}, y) \approx \widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)$. Further, this implies that, if fitting a simple linear regression $\hat{y} = \beta_0 + \beta_1 y + \epsilon$, then the fitted slope would satisfy $\hat \beta_1 =\widehat{\mathrm{Cov}}(\hat y,y)/\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y) \approx 1$ (assuming $\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)>0$), which is exactly the first-order behavior we aim to achieve with the EV-awareness problem.

\paragraph{Division of the log-prices (normalized log prediction).}
Let us assume the prices satisfy $P > 1$, thus $y>0$. Let the assessment ratios proxy $g$ in (\ref{opt:general_learner_constrained}) and (\ref{opt:general_learner_penalized}) in this case be
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:g_div}
g_{\texttt{div}}(\hat y,y):=\frac{\hat y}{y}.
\end{equation}
\todo{Create a notation for  the centered $y_c$, to use everywhere} Then
% with small $\epsilon>0$ (stability). Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_div}
C_{\texttt{div}}(f)
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{\hat y_i}{y_i}\,(y_i - \Bar{y}) 
=\frac{1}{n}\langle  r, y_c \rangle,
\qquad \text{ with } r_i:=\frac{\hat{y}_i}{y_i}.
\end{equation}
Thus $g_{\texttt{div}}$ imposes linear independency between the assessed ratios and the centered log-price vector $y_c$, a homogeneous moment (a single projection of $\hat{ y}$). That is, if $C_{\texttt{div}}(f)\approx 0$, we are enforcing orthogonality between $\hat{y}$ and the vector of weights $w^{\texttt{div}}$,  which gives more attention to: (1) those samples far away from the mean, but even more if the prices are low (where the general mass of data points is). 

\subsection{Global-coupling structure (model-agnostic)}
\label{sec:structure}

As one can note from the covariance penalization term definition, $C_g(f)$ is a scalar value that depends on all the observations in the dataset $\D$, i.e., it is a global-coupling constraint/regularization term. The problem is, {\todo{add a quote for this} in the general learner case, we would like to have no interaction (no coupling) of the gradients and hessians of the loss function; specially when enforcing constraints}. Relevant examples of this are Gradient Boosting methods and Neural Networks. Thus, the necessity for revising the nature of the coupling behavior of $C_g(f)$ in the following lemma. 

% The following statement formalizes why the penalty is compatible with boosting and neural networks.
% Define the lemma environment
\newtheorem{lemma}{Lemma} 
\begin{lemma}[Affine-in-prediction moments yield rank-one coupling]
\label{lem:rank_one}
Assume $g(\hat y,y)=a(y)\hat y+b(y)$ for some functions $a,b$. Define weights
$w_i:=a(y_i)\,y_{c,i}$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:linear_form}
c_g(f)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n w_i \hat y_i \;+\; \underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n b(y_i)\,y_{c,i}}_{\text{constant w.r.t. }f}.
\end{equation}
For the squared penalty $\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2$, the gradient in prediction space is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:grad_general}
\frac{\partial}{\partial \hat y_i}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2\Bigr)
=\frac{\rho}{n}\,c_g(f)\,w_i,
\end{equation}
and the (prediction-space) Hessian equals the dense rank-one matrix
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hess_general}
\nabla^2_{\hat{\mathbf y}}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2\Bigr)=\frac{\rho}{n^2}\,\mathbf w\mathbf w^\top.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\noindent
\emph{Instantiation.}
For \texttt{diff}, $a(y)\equiv 1$ and $b(y)=-y$, so $w_i=y_{c,i}$ and the constant term in
\eqref{eq:linear_form} equals $-\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)$, recovering \eqref{eq:cov_diff}.
For \texttt{div}, $a(y)=1/y^\dagger$ and $b(y)\equiv 0$, yielding $w_i=y_{c,i}/y_i^\dagger$ and
\eqref{eq:cov_div}.

\hrule



\section{Covariance-penalized learning for vertical equity}
\label{sec:cov_penalty_general}

\paragraph{Setup.}
Given data $\{(x_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ with $y_i=\log(P_i)$, let $\mathcal H$ be a hypothesis class and
$f\in\mathcal H$ produce log-predictions $\hat y_i:=f(x_i)$.
Write $\bar y:=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n y_i$ and $y_{c,i}:=y_i-\bar y$.

\paragraph{Baseline risk.}
We consider
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:base_obj}
\min_{f\in\mathcal H}\quad
\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2 \;+\;\Omega(f),
\end{equation}
where $\Omega$ is a model-dependent regularizer.

\paragraph{Equity moment and formulations.}
Let $g:\mathbb R\times\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be a transform and define the centered cross-moment
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:moment_def}
c_g(f)\;:=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n g(\hat y_i,y_i)\,y_{c,i}.
\end{equation}
Since $\sum_i y_{c,i}=0$, \eqref{eq:moment_def} equals the empirical covariance
$\widehat{\mathrm{Cov}}(g(\hat y,y),y)$ (full-sample).
We study either the constrained problem
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:constrained}
\min_{f\in\mathcal H}\ \frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2+\Omega(f)
\quad \text{s.t.}\quad |c_g(f)|\le \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
or the penalized surrogate
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:penalized}
\min_{f\in\mathcal H}\ \frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\hat y_i)^2+\Omega(f)
\;+\;\frac{\rho}{2}\,\phi\!\bigl(c_g(f)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where typically $\phi(c)=c^2$ (two-sided) or $\phi(c)=[c]_-^2$ (one-sided).\footnote{One-sided penalties
are nonsmooth at $0$; a smooth approximation (e.g., Huber) can be used when needed.}
In nonconvex classes, \eqref{eq:penalized} is interpreted as a practical surrogate (no guaranteed
$\rho\leftrightarrow\varepsilon$ mapping).

\subsection{Two moments used in practice: \texttt{diff} and \texttt{div}}
\label{sec:diff_div}

\paragraph{\texttt{diff} (log assessment ratio).}
Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:g_diff}
g_{\texttt{diff}}(\hat y,y):=\hat y-y=\log(\hat P/P).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_diff}
c_{\texttt{diff}}(f)
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (\hat y_i-y_i)\,y_{c,i}
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \hat y_i\,y_{c,i} - \widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y),
\quad
\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n y_{c,i}^2.
\end{equation}
If $\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)>0$, the OLS calibration slope of $\hat y$ on $y$ (with intercept) is
$\hat\beta=\widehat{\mathrm{Cov}}(\hat y,y)/\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)$, hence $c_{\texttt{diff}}(f)=0$
implies $\hat\beta=1$.

\paragraph{\texttt{div} (normalized log prediction).}
Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:g_div}
g_{\texttt{div}}(\hat y,y):=\frac{\hat y}{y^\dagger},
\qquad y^\dagger:=\max\{y,\epsilon\},
\end{equation}
with small $\epsilon>0$ (stability). Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cov_div}
c_{\texttt{div}}(f)
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \frac{\hat y_i}{y_i^\dagger}\,y_{c,i}
=\frac{1}{n}\langle \mathbf w^{\texttt{div}},\hat{\mathbf y}\rangle,
\qquad w^{\texttt{div}}_i:=\frac{y_{c,i}}{y_i^\dagger}.
\end{equation}
Thus \texttt{div} imposes a homogeneous moment (a single projection of $\hat{\mathbf y}$), and should be
viewed as a reweighted global constraint rather than a direct constraint on $\hat P/P$.

\subsection{Global-coupling structure (model-agnostic)}
\label{sec:structure}

The following statement formalizes why the penalty is compatible with boosting and neural networks.

\begin{lemma}[Affine-in-prediction moments yield rank-one coupling]
\label{lem:rank_one}
Assume $g(\hat y,y)=a(y)\hat y+b(y)$ for some functions $a,b$. Define weights
$w_i:=a(y_i)\,y_{c,i}$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:linear_form}
c_g(f)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n w_i \hat y_i \;+\; \underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n b(y_i)\,y_{c,i}}_{\text{constant w.r.t. }f}.
\end{equation}
For the squared penalty $\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2$, the gradient in prediction space is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:grad_general}
\frac{\partial}{\partial \hat y_i}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2\Bigr)
=\frac{\rho}{n}\,c_g(f)\,w_i,
\end{equation}
and the (prediction-space) Hessian equals the dense rank-one matrix
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hess_general}
\nabla^2_{\hat{\mathbf y}}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{2}c_g(f)^2\Bigr)=\frac{\rho}{n^2}\,\mathbf w\mathbf w^\top.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\noindent
\emph{Instantiation.}
For \texttt{diff}, $a(y)\equiv 1$ and $b(y)=-y$, so $w_i=y_{c,i}$ and the constant term in
\eqref{eq:linear_form} equals $-\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(y)$, recovering \eqref{eq:cov_diff}.
For \texttt{div}, $a(y)=1/y^\dagger$ and $b(y)\equiv 0$, yielding $w_i=y_{c,i}/y_i^\dagger$ and
\eqref{eq:cov_div}.

\subsection{Practical notes (kept minimal)}
\label{sec:practical_min}

\paragraph{Scaling.}
To reduce dependence of $\rho$ on the scale of $y$ and the choice of $g$, one may penalize a standardized
moment (e.g., $c_g(f)/\widehat{\mathrm{Std}}(y)$) or an empirical correlation.

\paragraph{Computation.}
Because $c_g(f)$ is global, exact evaluation uses all data (given predictions) and matches
\eqref{eq:penalized}. Mini-batch estimates generally optimize a different objective due to loss of
global centering; a common compromise is periodic full-sample refresh of $c_g(f)$ during training.


\hrule

Thus, we constraint ourselves to three cases of interest: linear regression, gradient boosting, and neural network models. 

\subsection{Linear Regression}
The linear regression idea is to give a baseline that is \todo{re-think this part} convex, has a closed-form solution, is interpretable, and which behavior and trade-offs are trackable. The VE-constrained problem (\ref{opt:general_learner}) in this case can be formulated as 
\begin{align}
    {\everymath{\displaystyle}
    \begin{array}{crl}
    f_{VE}(\varepsilon):= &  \arg\min_{\beta \in \R p}  &  \|y - X\beta\|_2^2 + \mathcal{R}(\beta)\\
       & s.t. & \left|\widehat{\text{Cov}}(r, y)\right| \leq \varepsilon ,
    \end{array}
    }
    \label{opt:linear_general_learner}
\end{align}
where $r=X\beta / y$, since the linear regression mapping is a parametric model where $f(X;\beta)=X\beta$. Note that, if we use $\ell_2$ regularization, then we can re-write the problem as an equivalent covariance-regularized problem, which lets us derive a closed-form solution in this case. Let the regularized problem be 
\begin{align}
    {\everymath{\displaystyle}
    \begin{array}{crl}
    f_{VE}(\rho_\varepsilon):= &  \arg\min_{\beta \in \R p}  &  \|y - X\beta\|_2^2 + \lambda \|\beta\|_2^2 +\rho  \left(\widehat{\text{Cov}}(r, y)\right)2,
    \end{array}
    }
    \label{opt:linear_general_learner_penalized}
\end{align}% \newpage
and let $w$

\textbf{Objective Function:}
$$ J(\beta) = ||\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\beta||_2^2 + \lambda ||\beta||_2^2 + \rho \left[ \text{Cov}\left(\frac{\mathbf{X}\beta}{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y}\right) \right]^2 $$

\textbf{1. Linearity of the Covariance Term:}
Let $\mathbf{r} = \frac{\mathbf{X}\beta}{\mathbf{y}}$ (element-wise). The covariance is linear w.r.t $\beta$:
\begin{align*}
    \text{Cov}(\mathbf{r}, \mathbf{y}) &= \frac{1}{N} \sum (r_i - \bar{r})(y_i - \bar{y}) \\
    &= \frac{1}{N} \sum \frac{\mathbf{x}_i^T \beta}{y_i} (y_i - \bar{y}) \quad \text{(centering cancels } \bar{r} \text{)} \\
    &= \beta^T \underbrace{\left[ \frac{1}{N} \mathbf{X}^T \left( \mathbf{1} - \frac{\bar{y}}{\mathbf{y}} \right) \right]}_{\mathbf{v}} = \mathbf{v}^T \beta
\end{align*}

\textbf{2. Quadratic Form:}
Substitute back into $J(\beta)$. The squared covariance becomes a rank-1 quadratic term:
$$ J(\beta) = (\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\beta)^T (\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\beta) + \lambda \beta^T \beta + \rho \beta^T (\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T) \beta $$

\textbf{3. Gradient:}
Set $\nabla_\beta J(\beta) = 0$:
$$ -2\mathbf{X}^T (\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\beta) + 2\lambda \beta + 2\rho (\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T) \beta = 0 $$

\textbf{4. Solution (Normal Equations):}
Rearranging terms yields the linear system:
$$ (\mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{X} + \lambda \mathbf{I} + \rho \mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T) \beta = \mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{y} $$

$$ \beta^* = (\mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{X} + \lambda \mathbf{I} + \rho \mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T)^{-1} \mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{y} $$


Let the objective function be composed of the Mean Squared Error (MSE), a Ridge penalty, and a Fairness penalty:
$$ J(\beta) = \underbrace{||\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\beta||^2}_{\text{MSE}} + \lambda ||\beta||^2 + \rho \underbrace{(\mathbf{v}^T \beta)^2}_{P(\beta)} $$

where $P(\beta)$ is the squared covariance fairness penalty, and $\mathbf{v}$ is the fairness gradient vector. Let $C = \mathbf{v}^T \beta$ be the current covariance.

The optimal solution $\beta(\rho)$ satisfies the linear system:
$$ \mathbf{H}(\rho) \beta = \mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{y} $$
where the Hessian is $\mathbf{H} = \mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X} + \lambda\mathbf{I} + \rho\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T$.

\section*{1. Sensitivity of Coefficients}

We differentiate the equilibrium condition $\mathbf{H}\beta = \mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{y}$ with respect to $\rho$:
\begin{align*}
    \frac{d}{d\rho} (\mathbf{H}\beta) &= \frac{d}{d\rho} (\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{y}) \\
    \left( \frac{d\mathbf{H}}{d\rho} \right) \beta + \mathbf{H} \left( \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} \right) &= 0 \\
    (\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T) \beta + \mathbf{H} \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} &= 0
\end{align*}

Since $\mathbf{v}^T\beta = C$:
$$ C \mathbf{v} + \mathbf{H} \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} = 0 \implies \boxed{ \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} = -C \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v} } $$

\section*{2. Change in Fairness Penalty}

How does the fairness penalty $P = C^2$ change as we increase $\rho$?
\begin{align*}
    \frac{dP}{d\rho} &= \frac{d}{d\rho} (\mathbf{v}^T \beta)^2 = 2C \mathbf{v}^T \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} \\
    &= 2C \mathbf{v}^T (-C \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v}) \\
    &= \boxed{ -2C^2 (\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v}) }
\end{align*}
Since $\mathbf{H}$ is positive definite, this quantity is always negative. Increasing $\rho$ always improves fairness.

\section*{3. Change in MSE (Shadow Price)}

How much accuracy do we lose? Using the chain rule:
$$ \frac{d\text{MSE}}{d\rho} = (\nabla_\beta \text{MSE})^T \frac{d\beta}{d\rho} $$

At the optimum, the gradient sum is zero: $\nabla \text{MSE} + \nabla \text{Ridge} + \rho \nabla P = 0$.
Assuming $\lambda=0$ (no Ridge) for simplicity:
$$ \nabla \text{MSE} = -\rho \nabla P = -\rho (2C \mathbf{v}) $$

Substituting back:
\begin{align*}
    \frac{d\text{MSE}}{d\rho} &= [-\rho (2C \mathbf{v})]^T [-C \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v}] \\
    &= \boxed{ 2\rho C^2 (\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v}) }
\end{align*}

\section*{Conclusion}

The trade-off rate (Shadow Price) is the ratio of the change in MSE to the change in Fairness:
$$ \frac{d\text{MSE}}{dP} = \frac{2\rho C^2 (\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v})}{-2C^2 (\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{H}^{-1} \mathbf{v})} = -\rho $$

Thus, \textbf{$\rho$ is the instantaneous exchange rate}: at the optimum, gaining 1 unit of fairness costs exactly $\rho$ units of MSE.

\section{Our Fairness Approach: In-processing setting}

% \subsection{Sensitive feature}



\subsection{General Fairness Notions: Independence and Separation}

We first introduce two concepts discused by \cite{lee2020maximalcorrelationapproachimposing}; \textit{independence} and \textit{separation}. The main idea behind these Our ideas are basically based on the fact that we do not want out model's predictions $\hat{Y}=f(X)$ to be dependent of the sensitive attribute $d$ (independence), or at least not more correlated to it than the vector $Y$ can be (separation). The typical characterization of these concepts is the following. 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Independence:} The model's predictions ($\hat{Y}=f(X)$) should be statistically independent of the sensitive attribute ($D$). In other words, we aim to construct a mapping $f:\X \rightarrow \Y$ such that $\hat{Y} \perp D$.
    
    Common \underline{examples} of measures of independence in fairness literature are \textit{Demographic Parity} (or Statistical Parity) and \textit{Predictive Parity}. 
    
    \item \textbf{Separation:} The model's error rates (e.g., false positive/negative rates in the classification case) should be independent of the sensitive attribute. This can be rephrased to no correlation between residuals $R= Y - \hat{Y}$ and the sensitive attribute $D$, conditional on $Y$. In other words, we aim to find a mapping $f:\X \rightarrow \Y$ that satisfies $\hat{Y} \perp D \mid Y$ or, equivalently, $R \perp D \mid Y$. 

    Common \underline{examples} of measures of separation in fairness literature are \textit{Equalized Odds} and \textit{Equal Opportunity}. 
\end{itemize}


These definitions imply independence in the whole distributional sense (not just expected values or variances), in all the modes of the distribution, so it is not as easy to compute. However, for simplicity and to adapt to the common applications with real-world data, we can still focus on the easier terms to constrain, and then try a more complex relationship like the infinite modes with kernels or similar tools.

Note also that these two definitions are not necessarily compatible and may oppose each other in various applications, as discussed by \cite{lee2020maximalcorrelationapproachimposing}. Thus, we will focus on addressing the desired sense of fairness depending on each of the contexts presented in the following sections. 

\subsection{Extension of Fairness Notions: Generalized Independence and Separation}
To have an even broader general fairness sense definition and to adapt properly to the real-world applications discussed in this work, we extend the definitions of \textit{independence} and \textit{separation} to the following ones. 

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Generalized Independence:} The distribution of the transformed score $g(\hat{Y}, Y)$, where $g$ is a context-dependent function, must be independent of the sensitive attribute $D$. In other words, we aim to construct a mapping $f:\X\rightarrow \Y$ such that $g(\hat{Y}, Y) \perp D$.

    \item \textbf{Generalized Separation:} The distribution of the transformed score $g(\hat{Y}, Y)$, where $g$ is a context-dependent function, must be independent of the sensitive attribute $A$, conditional on the target variable $Y$. In other words, we aim to construct a mapping $f:\X\rightarrow \Y$ such that $g(\hat{Y}, Y) \perp D \;|\; Y$.
\end{itemize}

As with the typical definitions, these are distributional independence, so they are hard to address and constraint in an in-processing context. 

\subsubsection{Case of Study: Property Taxation Fairness Notions}


\begin{itemize}
    \item {Vertical Equity Measures (Independence)} 
    
     In this context, the \qt{sensitive attribute} $D$ is the True Price $Y$ itself or its inverse $1/Y$, and the function $g$ is the ratio $g(\hat{Y}, Y)={\hat{Y}}/{Y}$. We aim to get an assessment model that satisfies the independence $$g(\hat{Y}, Y)\perp Y \iff \hat{Y}/Y\perp Y.$$ 
     If the model satisfies this condition, then we have no \textit{regressivity} nor \textit{progressivity} in the assessment model. The usual metrics that Assessment Officers ({\color{red} cite IAAO}) use typically include 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Price-related Differential (PRD), defined as $\Expected{\hat{Y}/Y}/ (\Expected{\hat{Y}} / \Expected{Y})$.
        \item \ldots
        % \item Price-related Bias (PRB), defined as $\Expected{\hat{Y}/Y}/ (\Expected{\hat{Y}} / \Expected{Y})$.
    \end{itemize}

    \item {Horizontal/Uniform Equity Measures (Separation)}
    
 In this context, the \qt{sensitive attribute} $D$ is usually a demographic feature like the type of area where the property is (urban residential, rural residential, seasonal, etc. See {\color{red} cite IAAO}) and the function $g$ could also depend on the ratio, like $g(\hat{Y}, Y)=|{\hat{Y}}/{Y}-c|$ (with $c\approx 1)$. We aim to get an assessment model that satisfies the conditional independence $$g(\hat{Y}, Y)\perp D\;|\; Y \iff  |\hat{Y}/Y-c|\perp D \;|\; Y.$$
      If the model satisfies this condition, then we have no \textit{regressivity} uniformity of the assessment with our model on the demographic groups. The usual metrics that Assessment Officers ({\color{red} cite IAAO}) use typically include 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Coefficient of Dispertion (COD), defined as $\frac{100}{\textbf{median}(\hat{Y}/Y)} \Expected{|\hat{Y}/Y - \textbf{median}(\hat{Y}/Y)|} $.
        % \item Price-related Bias (PRB), defined as $\Expected{\hat{Y}/Y}/ (\Expected{\hat{Y}} / \Expected{Y})$.
        \item \ldots
    \end{itemize}
    
\end{itemize}

{\color{red} PROPERLY THINK AND DEFINE THESE GENERALIZED FORMS.}


\subsection{Approach for Achieving Independence}

Let $X\in \R^{n\times p}$ be a matrix of observable features, $y \in \R^n$ the target vector to predict, and $d \in \R^n$ a sensitive attribute which we can \textbf{access only during the training} stage (e.g. membership to $q$-quantile group of $y$, legal constraint to not include $d$ on $X$, etc.) 

\subsubsection{Linear Constrained and Penalized Models}

We are going to compare three methods for constraining/penalizing some of the fairness metrics that we are interested in using linear models, i.e. we estimate $y$ as linear combination of the features on $X$ as $f(X)=X\beta$. As a weak proxy of the condition $g(\hat{Y}, Y) \perp D$, we start by constraining the empirical covariance between them, i.e., we impose that our model has to satisfy \begin{equation}
\label{ineq:empirical_covariance}
|Cov(g(\hat{y}, y), d)| \leq \varepsilon \iff \frac{1}{n}\left |\sum_{i=1}^n (g(\hat{y}_i, y_i) - {\boldsymbol\mu}(g(\hat{y}, y)) )(d_i - {\boldsymbol\mu}(d))\right| \leq \varepsilon,    
\end{equation}
for some $\varepsilon > 0$ and ${\boldsymbol\mu}$ is the average of the values of a vector.\\ 
{\color{red} (JUSTIFY THE THREE ROBUST POVS OF STABLE REGRESSION FOR GENERALIZATION)}\\
We explore three ways of constraining/regularizing our linear model to satisfy (\ref{ineq:empirical_covariance}), which include
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Covariance-Constrained Adversarial Regression.} This means directly imposing the constraint on all the samples, by modifying the modeling of the original Stable Regression and solving % Stable Regression
    % \begin{align*}
    %     \begin{array}{cc}
    %         \min_{\beta} & \max_{w \in \Delta_n^{(r)}}  \\
    %          & 
    %     \end{array}
    % \end{align*} 
{\everymath{\displaystyle}
\begin{align*}
    \begin{array}{rrll}
    \P_1(K, \varepsilon, \lambda):=&  \min_{\beta}  & \max_{w\in \Delta_{n}^{(K)}} \left\{ \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \cdot|y_i - x_i\t\beta| \right\} + \lambda \mathcal{R}(\beta) &  \\
         & s.t. & \frac{1}{n}\left| \sum_{i=1}^n (g(\hat{y}_i, y_i) - \muBold{(g(\hat{y}, y})))(d_i - \muBold({d})) \right| \leq \varepsilon,\\
         =& \min_{\beta, \nu, \theta} & \nu + \frac{1}{K} \sum_{i=1}^n \theta_i  \\
         & s.t. &\left| \sum_{i=1}^n (g(\hat{y}_i, y_i) - \muBold{(g(\hat{y}, y})))(d_i - \muBold({d})) \right| \leq n \varepsilon ,\\
         && |y_i - x_i\t \beta| \leq \nu + \theta_i, & \forall i \in [n] \\
         && \theta \geq 0,
    \end{array}
\end{align*}}
where $\mathcal{R}$ is some regularization function of the parameter $\beta$ (e.g., $\ell_1$/$\ell_2$ regularization or both).

\textit{Note: The objective only focuses on the worst-case residual samples, but the constraint is over the whole sample of data points. The analogy with the squared residuals is also valid.} 
\item \textbf{Surrogate Covariance-Regularized Adversarial Regression.} Instead of focusing on the worst cases of residual magnitudes, we formulate an adversarial problem that focuses on the worst cases of a combination of residual magnitudes and covariance sub-terms (covariance-regularized). Since we cannot follow the same procedure as before by having negative quantities, we regularize the surrogate upper bound using the triangle inequality (i.e. $|a+b|\leq |a|+|b|$) to aim at constraining the sum of absolute sub-terms of the covariance. In other words, we solve 
    % \end{align*} 
{\everymath{\displaystyle}
\begin{align*}
    \begin{array}{rrll}
    \P_2(K, \rho, \lambda):=&  \min_{\beta} \max_{w\in \Delta_{n}^{(K)}} &\frac{1}{K} \left\{ \sum_{i=1}^n w_i \cdot\left(|y_i - x_i'\beta| + \rho|(d_i - \Bar{d})(\hat{y}_i - \overline{\hat{y}})| \right)\right\} + \lambda \mathcal{R}(\beta), & \\
    =& \min_{\beta, \nu, \theta, u,\ell,c,q} \quad & \nu + \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^n \theta_i \\
    & s.t. \quad  & u_i + l_i + \rho (c_i + q_i) \leq \nu + \theta_i,& \forall i  \\
    &   & y_i - \hat{y}_i = u_i - \ell_i, & \forall i  \\
    && (d_i - \Bar{d})(\hat{y}_i - \overline{\hat{y}}) = c_i - q_i, & \forall i\\
   &&  \theta, u, \ell, c, q \geq 0 \\
   & & 
    \end{array}
\end{align*}}
\textit{Note: Now the objective focuses on the worst-case of the combination of residual and covariance sub-terms, and the constraint could be invalid on some data points. The analogy with the squared residuals is still valid.} 

\item \textbf{Surrogate Covariance-Regularized Separable Adversarial Regression.} Same idea as before, but choosing the worst samples for residuals and a surrogate of covariance separately. In other words, solving 
{\everymath{\displaystyle}
\begin{align*}
    \begin{array}{rrll}
    \P_3(K, \rho, \lambda):=& \frac{1}{K}  \min_{\beta} \max_{w\in \Delta_{n}^{(K)}} & \left\{ \sum_{i=1}^n w_i |y_i - x_i'\beta|\right\} +  \rho \max_{w\in \Delta_{n}^{(K)}} \left\{\sum_{i=1}^n w_i|(d_i - \Bar{d})(\hat{y}_i - \overline{\hat{y}})| \right\} + \lambda \mathcal{R}(\beta), & \\
    =& \min_{\beta, \nu, \theta, u,\ell,c,q} \quad & \nu_1 + \frac{1}{K} \sum_{i=1}^n \theta^{1}_i + \rho \left(\nu_2 + \frac{1}{K} \sum_{i=1}^n \theta_{i}^2\right) \\
    & s.t. \quad  & u_i + l_i  \leq \nu_1 + \theta_{i}^1,& \forall i  \\
    & & c_i + q_i \leq \nu_2 + \theta_i^2,& \forall i  \\
    &   & y_i - \hat{y}_i = u_i - \ell_i, & \forall i  \\
    && (d_i - \Bar{d})(\hat{y}_i - \overline{\hat{y}}) = c_i - q_i, & \forall i\\
   &&  \theta, u, \ell, c, q \geq 0 \\
   & & 
    \end{array}
\end{align*}}
\textit{Note: Now the objective focuses separately on the worst-cases of the residual and covariance sub-terms, which do not necessarily match. The analogy with the squared residuals is still valid.} 

\end{enumerate}

\section{Robust Constrained Regression}

% Data: (x_i, y_i, s_i, v_i) for i=1,...,n
%   x_i \in R^p     features
%   y_i             response (e.g., sale price or log-sale-price, depending on your model)
%   s_i > 0         sale price used to form the ratio
%   v_i             value proxy used for the PRD (vertical equity) covariance surrogate
% Hyperparameters:
%   alpha \in (0,1]   stability level (keeps worst alpha-fraction)
%   kappa >= 0        PRD-surrogate tolerance (covariance bound)
%   sigma_max >= 0    COD-surrogate tolerance via variance bound
%   R(\beta)          optional regularizer, e.g., ||\beta||_1 or (1/2)||\beta||_2^2

% ----- (A) Min-max (stable LAD) statement -----
\begin{align}
\min_{\beta \in \mathbb{R}^p}\;\; 
&\max_{w \in \mathcal{W}_\alpha}\;\sum_{i=1}^n w_i\,|y_i - x_i^\top \beta|
\;+\;\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\,\mathcal{R}(\beta) \\
\text{s.t.}\;\;
&\left|\operatorname{Cov}\big(v,\; r(\beta)\big)\right|\le \kappa, \\
&\operatorname{Var}\big(r(\beta)\big)\le \sigma_{\max}^2,
\end{align}
where
\[
\mathcal{W}_\alpha := \left\{w \in \mathbb{R}^n_+:\; \sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 1,\;\; 0 \le w_i \le \frac{1}{\alpha n}\right\},
\qquad
r_i(\beta) := \frac{x_i^\top \beta}{s_i}.
\]

% ----- (B) Single minimization (CVaR dual form) -----
Introduce variables:
\[
t \in \mathbb{R},\quad
u_i \ge 0,\quad
p_i \ge 0,\quad
n_i \ge 0
\qquad (i=1,\dots,n).
\]
Then the problem is equivalent to:
\begin{align}
\min_{\beta,t,u,p,n}\;\;
& t + \frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^n u_i \;+\;\lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\,\mathcal{R}(\beta)
\label{eq:final}\\
\text{s.t.}\;\;
& y_i - x_i^\top \beta = p_i - n_i, \qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:lad1}\\
& u_i \ge (p_i+n_i) - t, \qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:cvar1}\\
& u_i \ge 0,\; p_i\ge 0,\; n_i\ge 0, \qquad i=1,\dots,n. \label{eq:nonneg}
\end{align}

% ----- PRD surrogate: covariance constraint on ratios -----
Define
\[
r_i(\beta) := \frac{x_i^\top \beta}{s_i},\qquad
\bar r(\beta) := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n r_i(\beta),\qquad
\bar v := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n v_i.
\]
Impose the (PRD-style) covariance bound:
\begin{align}
-\kappa \;\le\; \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (v_i-\bar v)\big(r_i(\beta)-\bar r(\beta)\big)\;\le\;\kappa.
\label{eq:cov}
\end{align}

% ----- COD surrogate: variance constraint on ratios -----
Impose the variance bound (COD surrogate):
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \big(r_i(\beta)-\bar r(\beta)\big)^2 \;\le\; \sigma_{\max}^2.
\label{eq:var}
\end{align}

% The final formulation is: minimize \eqref{eq:final} subject to
% \eqref{eq:lad1}--\eqref{eq:nonneg}, \eqref{eq:cov}, \eqref{eq:var}.

\section{Second Try}

%==========================
% Robust/stable LAD regression that is ALSO robust to (surrogate) PRD and COD
% via a SINGLE adversary w that selects the worst α-fraction of points.
%
% Key idea (tractability):
%   Put everything inside  max_{w∈Wα} ∑ w_i g_i(β),
%   where g_i(β) is a per-point composite “badness” score:
%       LAD residual  +  λ_PRD * |PRD-contribution_i|  +  λ_COD * (ratio-deviation_i)^2.
%
% This keeps the inner maximization linear in w (hence CVaR-dualizable).
%==========================

\paragraph{Data and constants.}
For $i=1,\dots,n$, let $x_i\in\mathbb{R}^p$, $y_i\in\mathbb{R}$, $s_i>0$ (sale price used for ratios),
and $v_i\in\mathbb{R}$ (value proxy for vertical equity).
Define the centered value proxy (fixed, not $w$-dependent)
\[
\bar v := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n v_i,
\qquad
\tilde v_i := v_i - \bar v.
\]
Choose a fixed ratio anchor $r_0$ (e.g., $r_0=1$ or the unweighted mean/median of historical ratios).

Define the model-implied ratio
\[
r_i(\beta) := \frac{x_i^\top \beta}{s_i},
\qquad
h_i(\beta) := r_i(\beta) - r_0.
\]

\paragraph{Adversary (stable/CVaR) uncertainty set.}
Fix $\alpha\in(0,1]$ and let
\[
\mathcal{W}_\alpha := \left\{w\in\mathbb{R}_+^n:\ \sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 1,\ \ 0\le w_i \le \frac{1}{\alpha n}\right\}.
\]

\paragraph{Per-point composite score (loss + constraint-violation surrogates).}
Let $\lambda_{\mathrm{PRD}}\ge 0$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm{COD}}\ge 0$ be tradeoff weights and define
\[
g_i(\beta)
:= |y_i - x_i^\top \beta|
\;+\;
\lambda_{\mathrm{PRD}}\ \big|\tilde v_i\, h_i(\beta)\big|
\;+\;
\lambda_{\mathrm{COD}}\ \big(h_i(\beta)\big)^2.
\]
(Here $|\tilde v_i h_i(\beta)|$ is a convex, per-point surrogate for “price-related differential/bias contribution,”
and $(h_i(\beta))^2$ is a convex per-point surrogate controlling dispersion of ratios, i.e., COD through variance.)

%---------------------------------------
% (A) Robust/stable composite objective (min-max)
%---------------------------------------
\paragraph{Robust/stable composite regression (min--max form).}
\begin{align}
\min_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}^p}\;
\max_{w\in\mathcal{W}_\alpha}\;
\sum_{i=1}^n w_i\, g_i(\beta)
\;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\mathcal{R}(\beta),
\label{eq:minmax}
\end{align}
where $\mathcal{R}(\beta)$ is any convex regularizer (e.g., $\|\beta\|_1$ or $\tfrac12\|\beta\|_2^2$).

%---------------------------------------
% (B) Single minimization via CVaR dualization
%     max_{w∈Wα} ∑ w_i g_i  =  min_{t,u} t + (1/(αn))∑ u_i  s.t. u_i ≥ g_i - t, u_i ≥ 0
%---------------------------------------
\paragraph{Single minimization (CVaR dual/epigraph form).}
Introduce variables
\[
t\in\mathbb{R},\quad u_i\ge 0,\quad
p_i\ge 0,\ n_i\ge 0,\quad
a_i\ge 0,\quad
b_i\ge 0
\qquad (i=1,\dots,n).
\]
Then \eqref{eq:minmax} is equivalent to the following single convex optimization problem:
\begin{align}
\min_{\beta,t,u,p,n,a,b}\quad
& t + \frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^n u_i \;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{reg}}\mathcal{R}(\beta)
\label{eq:final}\\
\text{s.t.}\quad
& y_i - x_i^\top \beta = p_i - n_i, \qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:lad_lin}\\
& e_i = p_i + n_i, \qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:e_def}\\
& h_i = \frac{x_i^\top \beta}{s_i} - r_0, \qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:h_def}\\[2mm]
& a_i \ge \tilde v_i\, h_i,\quad a_i \ge -\tilde v_i\, h_i,\qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:abs_prd}\\
& b_i \ge h_i^2,\qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:sq_cod}\\[1mm]
& u_i \ge \Big(e_i + \lambda_{\mathrm{PRD}} a_i + \lambda_{\mathrm{COD}} b_i\Big) - t,\qquad i=1,\dots,n, \label{eq:cvar_slack}\\
& u_i \ge 0,\ p_i\ge 0,\ n_i\ge 0,\ a_i\ge 0,\ b_i\ge 0,\qquad i=1,\dots,n. \label{eq:nonneg}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Conic representability of $b_i \ge h_i^2$ (optional).}
Constraint \eqref{eq:sq_cod} can be represented with a rotated second-order cone:
\[
(b_i,\ \tfrac12,\ h_i)\ \in\ \mathcal{Q}_r
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
b_i \ge h_i^2,\ b_i\ge 0,
\]
where $\mathcal{Q}_r := \{(x,y,z): x\ge 0,\ y\ge 0,\ 2xy \ge z^2\}$.

% The final "robust in every sense" model is \eqref{eq:final}--\eqref{eq:nonneg} (plus the cone for \eqref{eq:sq_cod} if desired).
\end{document}
